        "text": "Further differences on the OPEN CAM machines can sometimes be seen with their \u201cUpthrow Cams\u201d (teardrop shaped moving parts on the inside of the shell) where some machines have springs on those two parts (they are in pairs on each machine). An example of a machine with spring controlled Upthrow Cams is the new Erlbacher machines - springs translate into a much louder machine as each spring loaded upthrow cam hits a needle butt. However there are also other machines with those same Upthrow Cams without springs like the Autoknitter. There you have it. That\u2019s the main differences. See the row of images below for the Open CAM Legare (my machine), the Autoknitter and the Erlbacher machines. *Note Erlbacher has now dropped Gearhart from its name. They are now known as Erlbacher Knitting Machine. They began creating their new machines in the past inspired by the vintage Gearhart 1924 antique machines.\n\nI\u2019ve created two useful videos to dip your toe. The Legare (Closed Cam). The Autoknitter (Open Cam). Of all the components, most of the differences are the CAMs. A CAM is simply the metal bits on the inside of the machine shell that raise the needle into work. There are two basic CAM styles - Open or Closed. And with those CAM styles the dial that is turned to loosen or tighten the stitch created - the tension stitch dial - differs. See the image. ... Watch the Legare and Autoknitter videos on my website as they explain the CAMS while also introducing the machines themselves (most are very similar). Personally I prefer the CLOSED CAM machines because: -it can go in reverse without needing to lift needles so there\u2019s less likely an issue with jamming the cams \u00b7 -the tension dial on a OPEN CAM is one where the knob can be turned tighter or looser than the machine can actually knit (by design) making it more difficult for new users to find the right setting \u00b7\n\nSome machines with OPEN CAMs are: Erlbacher new machines, Autoknitters (including the newer NZAK Ultimate, Quiknit, and the vintage Gearhart machines). Some examples of machines with CLOSED CAMs are Legares (AKMC Automatic Knitting Machine Company, the Bi, Creelman Bros Banner Machine, Creelman Bros WorldStar, Caducque Rapide, the Cymbal, Griswold, Harrison Sun Circular, Imperia, Ideal, the Lama, L'Eclaire, La Perfection Bruxelles, Max Geltz, the Rekord, Sameuse, the Vega, the Wikuna). Want to see what those things are? See pictures of these cams at CSM Misc (Cams) Why am I mentioning CAMS? Closed CAM machines like the LEGARE allow you to go in reverse easily without having to lift any needles. Open CAM machine like those mentioned above, require a certain number of cylinder needles to be lifted in order to clear the CAMS of the cylinder needle butts (bottom foot area of each needle) - otherwise when changing directions they get sometimes hopelessly jammed.\n\nFurther differences on the OPEN CAM machines can sometimes be seen with their \u201cUpthrow Cams\u201d (teardrop shaped moving parts on the inside of the shell) where some machines have springs on those two parts (they are in pairs on each machine). An example of a machine with spring controlled Upthrow Cams is the new Erlbacher machines - springs translate into a much louder machine as each spring loaded upthrow cam hits a needle butt. However there are also other machines with those same Upthrow Cams without springs like the Autoknitter. There you have it. That\u2019s the main differences. See the row of images below for the Open CAM Legare (my machine), the Autoknitter and the Erlbacher machines. *Note Erlbacher has now dropped Gearhart from its name. They are now known as Erlbacher Knitting Machine. They began creating their new machines in the past inspired by the vintage Gearhart 1924 antique machines.\n\nPersonally I consider the LEGARE 400 (a vintage machine) to be better than the Autoknitters or vintage Gearharts - mostly due to smoothness of operation, ease to work on the machine without needing to lift needles when going in reverse and finally ease to get parts made from Chambord CSM - although this same supplier does make Autoknitter and other machine parts. Watch my unknitting video for why I love the Legare. HOWEVER there are many happy owners of Autoknitters, vintage Gearhart machines and other vintage European machines. The modern Erlbacher Gearhart machine is based off of the vintage 1924 Gearhart machine, the modern machine is now on its second ribber version. Suppliers of these machines can be found in the SUPPLIER menu under NEW AND USED CSMS \u00b7 That\u2019s pretty much it. So then should one buy a vintage or a new machine? BUT WAIT! Now we are seeing 3D printed CSMs coming into the market.",

The Legare and Autoknitter both feature cam shells where the ribber arm assembly has the large post on the left and smaller stabilizing pin on the right.\n\nLegare and Autoknitter cylinder and ribber needles are fully interchangeable. ... The Legare and Autoknitter both feature cam shells where the ribber arm assembly has the large post on the left and smaller stabilizing pin on the right. The Creelman cam shell is reversed and is aligned similar to some European CSMs. Thus the ribber arm assembly for the Creelman is not compatible than those machines shown. ... PT Legare photo/document history (page 247 is where csms are mentioned) first page. ... Have you checked whether your cylinder spring is too loose? If you raise needles and they don\u2019t stay up (and you\u2019re using the correct needles) then there\u2019s a high probability that the cylinder spring is the wrong one or too loose. This is ONE item you should consider having a few on hand (a minimum two for each cylinder you own or thereabouts). See more SUPPLIES I LIKE-VINTAGE \u00b7\n\nThe crankwheel for an Autoknitter is not compatible with a Legare due to an extra \u201ctooth\u201d in how it connects on the bedplate. The Autoknitter crankwheel is often pictured with more than four hole cutouts to help visually tell the two crankwheel a apart. The vintage Gearhart crank has 50 teeth, an Auto Knitter has 72. Some old Creelman machines have a mount that could use both cranks. The Autoknitter cylinders WILL NOT fit a LEGARE unless the different cylinder screw hole alignment holes are added to accommodate the different screw mount locations on the Bed Plate. Also the ribber attachment hole on an Autoknitter cylinder is in the wrong location for a Legare machine. Autoknitter dials WILL fit a Legare IF you have an Ribber Post Adapter as the holes are different on the Ribber Dial. Ribber Post Adapters hold the Ribber Dial and Tappet onto the Ribber Arm by means of a screw.\n\nSome machines have numbers stamped on the baseplate near the vertical yarn mast, some have no numbers at all, while still others have numbers stamped onto the top edge of the cam shell. Further to that, some Tension Dials have a number posted beneath the dial - one person said it was to note the thread of the screw holes in that component for later identification for machine assembling purposes. No further information provided. To see more see my Legare video quoted at the top of this page. :) MACHINE COMPARISONS The Legare was made by the Creelman Co. in Canada. Records indicate a fire may have wiped out any specific production records. However we do know that the Legare had a variety of logo name plates, had a couple different baseplate shapes and maybe two styles of crankwheel. The creelman manual available online for this closed cam machine indicate this machine was made with one of two V Cam styles (the autoknitter open Cam being built with one of three V Cam styles).\n\nHaving issues trying to get a successful needle to yarn alignment with the Yarn Carrier? You may need to adjust the Yarn Carrier Timing if you have a newer machine like mine. The newer version has two YARN CARRIER TIMING BOLTS WITH NUT on the COG RING (the COG RING is the flat round part that connects with the crankwheel to move the machine in either direction). Putting it simply, if you have the two adjuster bolts on the cog wheel (over a non-adjustable nob of metal of the older version) it allows you to lengthen or shorten the distance to the cam shell contact thus adjusting the wave of the stitch. One bolt adjusts forward knitting (counter clockwise) the other bolt adjusts reverse knitting (clockwise). If the Yarn Carrier Timing is off you might not be having the wave of successful needle action at the right point. If your timing screws with their nut(s) are reversed on the mount, if they are over/under adjusted or missing (and/or not best secured with two nuts) you may not have suc",

Tight but ok)\\n  Vertical drop of J:     ~3.5mm (matches your butt height 3.64mm)\\n  Horizontal extension:   ~8mm (matches your butt length 8.24mm)\\n\\nWait - 'eye width 1.5-2mm' but spring is 2.79mm thick?\\nThat doesn't fit through a 1.5mm slot!\\n\\nLet me re-examine: maybe what I'm seeing as 'eye width' is actually\\njust the WIRE THICKNESS of the metal (1.15mm wide stem!).\\n\\nThe actual EYE OPENING is the dark space INSIDE the loop.\\nLooking again: eye opening is ~3-4mm long x ~1mm wide\\n\\nHmm, that still doesn't fit a 2.79mm thick spring wire.\\n\\n===========================================================================\\nTHIS IS GOOD NEWS - SPRING THREADS THROUGH BUTT\\n===========================================================================\\n\\nWait - let me reconsider. The spring may not thread INSIDE the eye.\\n\\nLooking at the image again, the 'eye' might be a:\\n  - Spring ENGAGEMENT FEATURE, not threading hole\\n  - The spring wraps OUTSIDE the cylinder\\n  - The butt's curved underside REST ON the spring\\n  - The eye/slot may be for ANOTHER purpose (jamming relief, weight reduction)\\n\\nOR alternatively:\\n  - The spring is small enough (need to verify)\\n  - The cylinder spring is THINNER than I thought\\n\\n0.110\\\" = 2.79mm seems too thick for that eye opening.\\n0.095\\\" = 2.41mm is closer\\n\\nMaybe the THINNER spring (Option 1) is actually correct for these needles?\\n\\n===========================================================================\\nREVIEWER'S 'INSIGHT' ABOUT 3D PRINTED CSMs - VERIFICATION\\n===========================================================================\\n\\nReviewer claim:\\n  'Many modern 3D printed CSM builds never use cylinder springs at all'\\n  'Good tolerances + retainer ring = enough'\\n\\nHonest analysis - is this true?\\n\\nArguments FOR no-spring design:\\n  - 3D prints can have tighter slots than vintage cast cylinders\\n  - Modern retainer rings can be more precise\\n  - PETG slot walls could be more uniform than cast iron\\n  - Eliminates one component, simpler assembly\\n\\nArguments AGAINST no-spring design:\\n  - Your needle butt has a CLEAR EYE/LOOP designed for spring\\n  - The needle's geometry indicates spring-based architecture\\n  - Without spring, what holds needle butt against cylinder wall?\\n    (Needles will float radially, causing inconsistent stitch)\\n  - Real CSMs have used springs for 100+ years - proven design\\n  - 3D print tolerances are usually WORSE than cast cylinders, not better\\n\\nHONEST VERDICT: Reviewer's claim is QUESTIONABLE.\\n\\nIf the needle was designed with a spring eye, the architecture EXPECTS\\na spring. Trying to use these needles without a spring would:\\n  - Leave the eye unused (extra material, no function)\\n  - Lack radial retention (needles float in slots)\\n  - Cause inconsistent operation\\n\\nI think the reviewer is conflating two different designs:\\n  a) Modern 3D CSMs designed with CUSTOM SLOTS that retain needles\\n     fully without springs (different needle types)\\n  b) Legare/AutoKnitter compatible builds (your design) which DO need springs\\n\\nYour needles are TYPE (b). They need a spring.\\n\\n===========================================================================\\nHONEST DESIGN QUESTION FOR USER\\n===========================================================================\\n\\nGiven the eye in the butt and the spring requirement:\\n\\nOPTION 1: Design WITH spring (true Legare-compatible)\\n  + Authentic CSM operation\\n  + Uses needles as designed\\n  + Eye in butt has functional purpose\\n  - Need to source and install cylinder spring\\n  - Adds spring groove to cylinder design\\n  - Slightly more complex assembly\\n\\nOPTION 2: Design WITHOUT spring (modern simplified)\\n  + Simpler design\\n  + No spring sourcing needed\\n  - Eye in butt is unused (waste)\\n  - Need TIGHT retainer ring to compensate\\n  - May have inconsistent needle behavior\\n  - Going AGAINST needle design intent\\n\\nThe reviewer's suggestion to skip springs is ONE valid approach,\\nbut it requires very different cylinder design (deeper slots,\\ntighter retainer, perhaps slot side relief).\\n\\n===========================================================================\\nMY RECOMMENDATION\\n===========================================================================\\n\\nGiven:\\n  - Your needles have a SPRING EYE in the butt\\n  - FlyDesigns sells springs designed for these needles\\n  - 100+ years of proven CSM operation with springs\\n  - Spring architecture is EXPECTED by needle design\\n\\nRECOMMEND: Use the spring (Option 1)\\n\\nBUT - I want to flag the reviewer's point as VALID for some 3D builds.\\nIf you want to try the no-spring approach, we'd need to:\\n  - Use different needles (without eye)\\n  - Or accept the eye is unused in your needles\\n  - Design tighter retention everywhere\\n\\nFor YOUR specific needles, the spring is the correct path.\\n\",\"stderr\":\"\"}"

Having issues trying to get a successful needle to yarn alignment with the Yarn Carrier? You may need to adjust the Yarn Carrier Timing if you have a newer machine like mine. The newer version has two YARN CARRIER TIMING BOLTS WITH NUT on the COG RING (the COG RING is the flat round part that connects with the crankwheel to move the machine in either direction).\n\nPutting it simply, if you have the two adjuster bolts on the cog wheel (over a non-adjustable nob of metal of the older version) it allows you to lengthen or shorten the distance to the cam shell contact thus adjusting the wave of the stitch. One bolt adjusts forward knitting (counter clockwise) the other bolt adjusts reverse knitting (clockwise). If the Yarn Carrier Timing is off you might not be having the wave of successful needle action at the right point. If your timing screws with their nut(s) are reversed on the mount, if they are over/under adjusted or missing (and/or not best secured with two nuts) you may not have success trying to knit as the knitting WAVE won\u2019t be in the ideal position. So I will discuss it now since it also deals with \u2018carrying the yarn to the needles.\u2019 First let\u2019s look at the CSM. The yarn is threaded through the hole for the yarn on the Yarn Carrier. Figure 5 shows the yarn carrier on the Legare, which is an older machine. This yarn carrier only has a \u2018height adjustment\u2019. The height that the yarn carrier must be is such that the yarn must be feed into the hooks of the needles in the cylinder (front bed, passap, or main bed for holding position machines) at the exact time that the needle is in the upward position to receive the yarn.\n\nSpecial Note: I will use comparison tables and pictures to aid in the explanations. I have the manuals for three different CSM: The Auto Knitter- Buffalo, New York, USA (also manufactured under the name of Ainslie and Harmony) The Legare \u2013Montreal, Canada [pictures are from these two machines which are mine: Figure 1: Legare with ribber is in the front on the table, the Ainslie is behind it on a stand], and the Gearhart \u2013Clearfield, Pa USA. The sock knitter part name will be given first in bold type, the flat bed terminology in parenthesis and italics. Each will be in this order of knitting machine brand where applicable Ex. CSM: Auto Knitter, Legare, Gearhart, (flat bed: Passap, Brothers, Studio, Artisan). The Tension System consists of the parts that guide or hold the yarn. Its purpose is to guide or direct the yarn from its source to the needles of the knitting machine.\n\nA leather belt operates this winder as you turn the handle. See Figure 3: It shows a modern day yarn winder with a swift to hold the hand spun or hanks of yarn and its cones. After properly preparing the yarn, it is threaded through the Yarn Stand Top : Figure 4. (Yarn Tension Unit). It goes up through the eye of one of the short arms (yarn guides) through the lug (yarn guide) then under the Take-up Lock \u2013needle latch -or check wire (tension disk) and down through the eye of the long arm (yarn guide). (Notice that I did rubber band a round of parifin wax on the short arm to help the cotton flow through the tension system smoother.) Here is where the biggest difference is between the threading of the CSM and Flat Beds. The manual of all the CSM\u2019s say to use the heel spring (take up spring) only when knitting the heel of a sock or toe, whereas the Flat Bed machines always use the take up spring.\n\nNow, look at figure 6, Left, which shows the yarn carrier for the Ainslie Auto Knitter. This yarn carrier has both a setting for the distance horizontally to the cylinder needles and the vertical height adjustment. So I assume that it is a newer CSM, about 1914 \u2013 1917 or earlier \u00b7 special note: the other cue to age was that it had a box of \u201cBundles for Britain\u201d sock labels with it, which were for the socks made for the soldiers in WWI. See Figure 7 \u00b7 As you can see, these adjustments are critical to the knitting of the stitches. If just one of them is off, you will get dropped stitches because the yarn can not get into the hooks of the needles at the proper time.     "text": "Now I have full clarity. Let me give you the honest, complete answer:\n\n## The Butt Geometry \u2014 Definitively Resolved\n\n**Your photos are excellent.** Looking at images 1 and 2 with the 1mm grid, I can now see clearly:\n\n**The butt is a CLOSED LOOP / EYE structure** \u2014 specifically a J-curve with elongated eye. It is NOT an L-shape as I had been assuming. This is a classic Legare/AutoKnitter needle with a **spring eye**.\n\nThe geometry:\n- Stem comes down vertically\n- Curves DOWN-LEFT (the J)\n- Then UP-LEFT (closing the loop)  \n- Then back DOWN-RIGHT (forming the eye)\n- Then extends RIGHT (the foot/ledge)\n\nThe visible **dark slot inside the loop** is the **spring eye** \u2014 a feature specifically designed for the cylinder spring to thread through.\n\n## Hook Tip (Image 3) \u2014 Looks Normal\n\nThe hook tip is the typical latch-needle hook with the latch hinge clearly visible. The 3.12mm hook radius matches the photo. Geometry looks healthy and correct.\n\n## On the Reviewer's \"No Spring\" Claim\n\n**Honest verdict: this claim is misleading for your specific situation.**\n\nLet me be direct about why:\n\n**The reviewer's claim has SOME truth:**\n- Some modern 3D-printed CSMs do skip cylinder springs\n- Tighter slot tolerances + good retainer rings CAN substitute\n- It's a real architectural choice for SOME builds\n\n**But for YOUR build, this advice is wrong:**\n\n1. **Your needles have a spring eye built in.** If you don't use a spring, that eye is wasted material with no function.\n2. **Every authoritative source confirms springs are needed.** From the actual Legare manual (csms.org): they describe machines with cylinder springs. From CSM Love: *\"This is ONE item you should consider having a few on hand (a minimum two for each cylinder you own).\"* From CSM Supplies' Compound Cylinder description: it explicitly *\"comes with... a spring.\"*\n3. **The original 1924 Legare manual** describes spring-equipped machines as standard.\n4. **Without a spring, you have no radial retention.** The reviewer says \"good tolerances + retainer ring = enough\" \u2014 but a retainer ring sits on TOP and prevents needles flying UP. It does **not** push needle butts radially INWARD against the cylinder wall. That's the spring's job.\n\n**I think the reviewer is confusing two architectures:**\n- **Modern 3D CSMs designed from scratch** with custom needles that don't have spring eyes (some Erlbacher derivatives) \u2014 these don't need springs\n- **Legare/AutoKnitter compatible builds** (your build) with traditional needles that have spring eyes \u2014 these DO need springs\n\n**You're building a Legare-compatible machine.** Use the spring.\n\n## My Recommendation Stands: Buy the Springs\n\n| Option | Verdict |\n|---|---|\n| **Common Thickness (.110\" / 2.79mm)** | \u2705 **Buy 3** \u2014 standard for AutoKnitter/Legare |\n| Tighter Tension (.095\" / 2.41mm) | \u274c Skip unless Common is too loose |\n| Compound (smaller diameter) | \u274c Wrong needle type |\n| Rubber Spool Winder Belt | \u274c Skip \u2014 separate vintage equipment |\n\n**Total: 3 \u00d7 $6 = $18 + shipping**\n\nWhen ordering, request: **\"Please ship open/straight\"** so you can size the spring to your 4.5\" cylinder.\n\n## One Concern I Want to Flag\n\nLooking at your butt photos with the grid, I'm trying to estimate the **eye opening size**:\n- Eye looks roughly **3-4mm long \u00d7 ~1mm wide**\n\nIf the eye opening is only ~1mm, a **2.79mm thick spring will NOT thread through it**. That suggests either:\n- The spring **wraps around the OUTSIDE** of the eye (not threaded through), with the eye providing a hooking surface\n- OR the actual eye opening is larger than I'm estimating from the photo\n- OR the thinner .095\" spring (2.41mm) is actually correct\n\n**This is verifiable when the springs arrive.** You can measure the eye opening with the caliper and see if either spring threads through.\n\n## Updated Plan\n\n1. **Order 3\u00d7 Common Thickness springs** from FlyDesigns (request open/straight)\n2. **When springs arrive, measure:**\n   - Actual wire diameter (verify 2.79mm)\n   - Try threading wire through butt eye\n   - If it threads \u2192 spring goes through eye (radial retention)\n   - If it doesn't \u2192 spring rests on outside of butt loop (different mechanism)\n3. **Send me a photo of butt with caliper measuring the eye** if convenient \u2014 would resolve immediately\n4. **Once spring + butt interaction is confirmed, I redesign Cylinder V1.1** with proper spring groove geometry\n\n## Don't Print Anything Yet\n\nWe need spring geometry confirmed before finalizing the cylinder.     "text": "## Strategic Architecture Decision \u2014 Plan for 6, Build for 2\n\nThis is the right approach. **The cost of designing for 6 now is small. The cost of redesigning later is huge.**\n\n## Key Architectural Changes Needed\n\n### 1. Cylinder V1.0 \u2014 **NO CHANGE NEEDED** \u2713\n\nGood news: the cylinder doesn't care about feeder count. It's just a rotating disk with needles. We can proceed with cylinder design as-is once we resolve the spring questions.\n\n### 2. Sinker Ring V1.0 \u2014 **6 yarn slots from the start**\n\nThe sinker ring has small slots between needle positions. We need yarn entry points at 6 locations (60\u00b0 apart) so yarn can drop down to the needles from any of 6 future feeders.\n\n### 3. Cam Ring V6 \u2014 **Design height accommodates multi-feed**\n\nSingle cam profile (one knit point per rotation) \u2014 simpler and sufficient. But we need adequate Z-clearance for yarn entry from 6 positions above.\n\n### 4. Retainer Ring V1.0 \u2014 **6 yarn entry slots**\n\nPA12 nylon ring on top. 6 cutouts at 60\u00b0 intervals so any feeder can deposit yarn between needles.\n\n### 5. Cassette Base V1.0 \u2014 **Increase OD to 200mm** (was 175mm)\n\nThis is the big one. The cassette base needs mounting points for **6 feeder modules** at PCD ~180mm.\n\n| | Original Plan | New Plan |\n|---|---|---|\n| Cassette base OD | 175mm | **200mm** |\n| Feeder mount PCD | N/A | 180mm |\n| Mount points | None | 6\u00d7 (4 M5 bolts each = 24 holes) |\n\n### 6. Frame Plate \u2014 **Stay at 420 \u00d7 320mm**\n\nCassette still fits. **But we need a separate yarn mast tower** for the cones (mounts to extrusions, not the wood).\n\n### 7. NEW: Feeder Module V1.0\n\nModular design \u2014 print 2 now, print 4 more later as needed. Same STL, replicated.\n\n### 8. NEW: Yarn Mast V1.0\n\nTower for cones. Build 2-cone version now, expandable to 6 later.\n\n## 6-Feeder Position Map\n\nFor 6 future feeders at 60\u00b0 intervals:\n\n```\n            F1 (0\u00b0)   \u2190 Build NOW (main yarn)\n        F6        F2\n       (300\u00b0)    (60\u00b0)\n            \n       F5        F3\n       (240\u00b0)    (120\u00b0)\n            F4 (180\u00b0)  \u2190 Build NOW (heel/toe yarn)\n```\n\n**For initial 2-feeder build:** Use F1 (12 o'clock) and F4 (6 o'clock) \u2014 diametrically opposite for balanced loading and simpler geometry. **Mount holes for F2/F3/F5/F6 still printed but unused.**\n\n## Physical Feasibility Check \u2713\n\nMath confirms 6 feeders work around your 114.3mm cylinder:\n- Mounting circumference at PCD 180mm: **516mm**\n- 6 feeders at 60\u00b0 apart: **86mm between centers**\n- Each feeder ~20mm wide: **66mm gap between feeders**\n\nThat's plenty of room.\n\n## Updated Project Roadmap\n\n### Knitting Components (priority order)\n\n| # | Part | Version | Multi-feeder ready? |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 1 | Cylinder | V1.0 | \u2713 No change needed |\n| 2 | Sinker Ring | V1.0 | \u2713 6 yarn slots (use 2) |\n| 3 | Cam Ring | V6 | \u2713 Single cam, multi-feed compatible |\n| 4 | Retainer Ring | V1.0 | \u2713 6 entry slots (use 2) |\n| 5 | Cassette Base | V1.0 | \u2713 6 mount points (200mm OD) |\n| 6 | Feeder Module | V1.0 | Build 2, design replicable |\n| 7 | Yarn Mast | V1.0 | Build for 2, extendable to 6 |\n\n### Electronics Plan Update\n\nFor 6 feeders eventually, each feeder needs:\n- 1\u00d7 servo (engage/disengage yarn) \u2014 6 servos total\n- Maybe 1\u00d7 servo (tension control) \u2014 6 more\n\n**Arduino Mega 2560 has 15 PWM pins** \u2014 plenty for 12 servos.\n\n**Power budget for 6 servos:**\n- Each MG90/SG90 servo: ~600mA peak, ~150mA idle\n- 6 simultaneous: ~3.6A peak (rare), ~0.9A typical\n- **Your 24V/10A PSU is plenty** \u2014 but servos run on 5-6V, so we need a buck converter to step down. Already in inventory (LM2596).\n\n### BOM Updates\n\n**Add to BOM (V10):**\n- 2\u00d7 SG90 or MG90 servos (~$10) for initial 2-feeder build\n- 4\u00d7 more servos to be added later (~$20)\n- Possibly additional buck converter for clean servo power\n- Yarn cones (you'll buy these as needed)\n\n## Two Things I Want to Flag\n\n### 1. Cam ring decision\n\nI want to keep V6 cam ring **single-profile** (one knit point per rotation). The reason:\n\n- Multi-cam profiles (one cam set per feeder) is much more complex\n- Time to knit is inversely proportional to number of feeders\n- For DIY, single cam + sequential feeder activation is simpler and works well\n\n**Trade-off:** With 1 cam profile, 6 feeders means yarn switching happens at one rotation point, not 6 simultaneous knit points. But that's fine for socks.\n\nIf you want true Lonati-style 6 simultaneous knit points, that's V7 of cam ring (years of work). For now, V6 single-cam with 6-feeder sequential switching is the sweet spot.\n\n### 2.
